Global well-posedness in the critical Besov space of the skew mean curvature flow in ℝ d : d ≥ 5 \mathbb{R}^{d}:d\geq 5
Ning-An Lai, Jie Shao, Zexian Zhang, Yi Zhou

Abstract
. In this paper we prove small-data global well-posedness for the skew mean curvature flow of codimension-two submanifolds of ℝ d + 2 \mathbb{R}^{d+2} ( d ≥ 5 d\geq 5 ) in the critical Besov space. With harmonic coordinates and Coulomb gauge, the flow is formulated as a quasilinear Schrödinger equation for the complex mean curvature coupled to an elliptic system for the geometric and gauge variables. The main difficulty is to control the frequency interactions at critical regularity, where no derivative margin is available. Our argument combines two complementary spacetime estimates derived from the mass and momentum balance laws: a new div-curl lemma introduced by the fourth author yields a bilinear estimate with a half-derivative gain, providing the key control of low-high interactions; while a quasilinear interaction Morawetz estimate provides critical spacetime bounds for comparable and high-high frequency interactions. These estimates coupled with the Gauss-Codazzi structure of the curvature equations yield the unique global solutions to the gauge-reduced system in the critical Besov space, and improves the previous small-data global regularity results.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 35Q55; Secondary: 35A01

1.
Introduction

The

skew mean curvature flow
(short for SMCF) is a distinguished dispersive geometric flow to describe the evolution of the oriented immersed codimension-two submanifolds. Let
(M^{d+2},\bar{g})
be an oriented
Riemannian manifold,
\Sigma^{d}
be an oriented
d
–dimensional manifold, and

F:[0,T)\times\Sigma\longrightarrow M

be a smooth one-parameter family of immersions. We write

\Sigma_{t}=F(t,\Sigma)
, denote by
g=F^{\ast}\bar{g}
the induced metric, and by

H(F)
the mean curvature vector of
\Sigma_{t}
. Since the normal bundle

N\Sigma_{t}
has rank two and is oriented, it admits a canonical complex structure

J(F)
, given by the positive rotation through
\pi/2
in each normal plane. The
SMCF can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{t}F=J(F)\mathbf{H}(F),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\Sigma,\\
F(0,x)=F_{0}(x).\end{cases}

(1.1)

Equivalently, and more conveniently for analysis, one may prescribe only the normal
component of the velocity:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle(\partial_{t}F)^{\perp}=J(F)\mathbf{H}(F),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T)\times\Sigma,\\
F(0,x)=F_{0}(x).\end{cases}

(1.2)

The latter formulation has a larger gauge group: time-dependent changes of
coordinates on
\Sigma_{t}
alter the tangential component of
\partial_{t}F
only and
therefore leave (
1.2
) unchanged.

The one-dimensional SMCF in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is actually the classical binormal or vortex-filament
flow. Its physical origin goes back to Da Rios
[
5
]
, who derived the localized motion
of a thin vortex filament in an ideal incompressible fluid. Hasimoto
[
10
]
subsequently
showed that, after a geometric transform, the filament equation changes into a
focusing cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation. This remarkable correspondence
explains the coexistence of geometric, Hamiltonian and dispersive structures in
SMCF and exhibits solitons and complete integrability in one dimension.

Higher-dimensional SMCF is the natural codimension-two analogue of the vortex-filament
dynamics. Shashikanth
[
32
]
related the two-dimensional SMCF to vortex membranes in
four-dimensional incompressible fluids, and Khesin
[
22
]
extended the local induction
picture to codimension-two vortex membranes in arbitrary dimension. SMCF also
arises in the asymptotic dynamics of vortices for Gross-Pitaevskii and related
Ginzburg-Landau models; and the rigorous connections were established, in particular, by
Lin
[
27
,
29
]
and Jerrard
[
21
]
in important geometric configurations.

From the PDE viewpoint, SMCF is a quasilinear Schrödinger-type geometric flow.
Unlike the parabolic mean curvature flow, it is nondissipative and Hamiltonian in
nature. Its study therefore sits at the intersection of geometric analysis,
dispersive PDE, vortex dynamics and gauge theory. A related central problem is to
understand whether small perturbations of a flat plane remain globally regular at
the regularity dictated by scaling. This question is especially delicate since
the Schrödinger evolution is coupled to nonlocal elliptic equations for the
metric, second fundamental form and gauge fields.

1.1.
Scaling and gauge freedom

If
F(t,x)
solves (
1.2
), then also

F_{\mu}(t,x)=\mu^{-1}F(\mu^{2}t,\mu x).

Consequently the scaling-critical homogeneous
Sobolev regularity for the immersion is

\dot{H}^{\frac{d}{2}+1}
,
whereas, since schematically
H=\Delta_{g}F
, the mean curvature and the complex
curvature variable
\psi
admit critical regularity

s_{c}=\frac{d}{2}-1
.

Gauge freedom is also central to the analysis. Formulation (
1.1
) is invariant
under time-independent reparametrizations, while (
1.2
) permits
time-dependent reparametrizations. In addition, the oriented normal frame may be
rotated by a phase. These two freedoms are used to impose harmonic coordinates on
the evolving surface and the Coulomb gauge on the normal connection in this paper, under which the geometric system becomes a quasilinear
Schrödinger equation coupled to an elliptic system, with no derivative loss at
the level required for the present argument.

1.2.
Known results

While we strive to present an overview of the pertinent results, a number of relevant publications could remain unaddressed given the bounds of our knowledge.
Da Rios
[
5
]
introduced the vortex-filament equation, and
Hasimoto
[
10
]
transformed it into the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger
equation. Geometric and Hamiltonian aspects of binormal and skew mean curvature motion
were developed in, among others, Gomez
[
8
]
, Haller–Vizman
[
9
]
,
Terng
[
38
]
, and Terng–Uhlenbeck
[
39
]
. Lin
[
27
,
29
]
and Jerrard
[
21
]
connected vortex
dynamics for Ginzburg–Landau/Gross–Pitaevskii type models with codimension-two
geometric motion. Shashikanth
[
32
]
and Khesin
[
22
]
related
higher-dimensional SMCF to vortex-membrane dynamics. Khesin–Yang
[
23
]

also exhibited explicit higher-dimensional examples, including finite-time
singular behavior, showing that unrestricted global regularity cannot hold.

For compact submanifolds, Song–Sun
[
33
]
proved local existence
for surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and Song
[
34
]
established local
existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence in arbitrary
dimensions. Huang–Tataru
[
14
,
15
]
exploited the enlarged
gauge freedom of the normal velocity formulation and developed low regularity
local theories. Li
[
25
,
26
]
established small-data global stability results for
Euclidean planes, together with scattering and wave-operator results under suitable
regularity assumptions. Huang-Li-Tataru
[
13
]
proved small-data global regularity for the skew mean curvature flow in the harmonic/Coulomb gauge for
d\geq 4
. Their result requires the initial mean curvature to be small in
H^{s_{d}}
, with

s_{d}\geq 3\quad\text{if }d=4,\qquad s_{d}>\frac{d+1}{2}+\frac{1}{2(d-1)}\quad\text{if }d\geq 5.

(1.3)

Since the scaling-critical index for the curvature is
s_{c}=d/2-1
, this remains two derivatives above scaling in dimension four and more than
3/2
derivatives above scaling in higher dimensions. Huang-Tataru
[
12
]
established local well-posedness of the skew mean curvature flow for large initial data in low-regularity Sobolev spaces for dimensions
d\geq 2
. More recently, Ifrim–Tataru
[
19
,
20
]
developed robust methods
for cubic quasilinear Schrödinger flows, including low-regularity and global
well-posedness, which provide an important broader perspective on the SMCF problem.

1.3.
The harmonic/Coulomb formulation

Following
[
14
,
13
]
, we use harmonic coordinates on the
evolving submanifold and impose the Coulomb condition on the normal
connection. We briefly recall the derivation of the
Schrödinger–elliptic system
(
1.16
)–(
1.18
).

We work in the Euclidean ambient space
(\mathbb{R}^{d+2},g_{\mathbb{R}^{d+2}})
and near a
flat
d
–plane. Write
F_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}F
and

g_{\alpha\beta}=F_{\alpha}\cdot F_{\beta},\qquad g^{\alpha\gamma}g_{\gamma\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}.

Choose an oriented orthonormal normal frame
(v_{1},v_{2})
satisfying

Jv_{1}=v_{2}
and
Jv_{2}=-v_{1}
, and introduce

m=v_{1}+\sqrt{-1}v_{2},\qquad\lambda_{\alpha\beta}=\kappa_{\alpha\beta}+\sqrt{-1}\tau_{\alpha\beta},\qquad\psi=g^{\alpha\beta}\lambda_{\alpha\beta}.

Here
\kappa
and
\tau
are the two real components of the second fundamental
form,
\lambda
is the complex second fundamental form, and
\psi
is the complex
mean curvature. The normal connection is represented by

A_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}v_{1}\cdot v_{2},\qquad B=\partial_{t}v_{1}\cdot v_{2},

while

V_{\gamma}=\partial_{t}F\cdot F_{\gamma}

records the tangential velocity generated by the choice of time-dependent
coordinates. Thus the geometric evolution (
1.2
) is encoded by the dispersive unknown

\psi
together with the elliptically determined geometric fields

\lambda,g,V,A,B
.

As mentioned above we impose harmonic
coordinates on
\Sigma_{t}
and Coulomb gauge condition

\displaystyle\nabla^{\alpha}A_{\alpha}=0.

(1.4)

We then may differentiate the basis to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{\alpha}F_{\beta}=\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}F_{\gamma}+\operatorname{Re}\left(\lambda_{\alpha\beta}\bar{m}\right),\\
\partial_{\alpha}^{A}m=-\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}F_{\gamma},\end{cases}

(1.5)

where
\partial_{\alpha}^{A}=\partial_{\alpha}+\sqrt{-1}A_{\alpha}
and
\nabla_{\alpha}^{A}=\nabla_{\alpha}+\sqrt{-1}A_{\alpha}
. The spatial compatibility conditions for (
1.5
) are the
Gauss–Codazzi–Ricci identities:

\displaystyle R_{\sigma\gamma\alpha\beta}=

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{Re}\left(\lambda_{\beta\gamma}\overline{\lambda}_{\alpha\sigma}-\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}\overline{\lambda}_{\beta\sigma}\right),

(1.6)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}^{A}\lambda_{\beta\gamma}=

\displaystyle\,\nabla_{\beta}^{A}\lambda_{\alpha\gamma},

(1.7)

and

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}A_{\beta}-\nabla_{\beta}A_{\alpha}=\operatorname{Im}\left(\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\overline{\lambda}_{\beta\gamma}\right).

(1.8)

Contracting the Gauss and Codazzi equations yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ric}_{\gamma\beta}=\operatorname{Re}\left(\lambda_{\gamma\beta}\overline{\psi}-\lambda_{\gamma\alpha}\overline{\lambda}^{\alpha}_{\beta}\right),

(1.9)

and

\displaystyle\nabla^{A,\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha\beta}=\nabla_{\beta}^{A}\psi.

(1.10)

In particular,
they preserve the exact tensorial structure of the Gauss term

\displaystyle(\lambda\wedge\lambda)_{\gamma\sigma}:=\lambda_{\gamma\sigma}\overline{\psi}-\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}\overline{\lambda}^{\alpha}_{\sigma},

(1.11)

which also appears in the metric equation in (
1.17
), and will be crucial in the subsequent high–high frequency analysis in Lemma
3.5
.

We now turn to the time evolution part. Since

\mathbf{H}=\operatorname{Re}\bigl(\psi\overline{m}\bigr),

the skew mean curvature flow (
1.2
) can be written as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F

\displaystyle=J(F)\mathbf{H}(F)+V^{\gamma}F_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Im}\bigl(\psi\overline{m}\bigr)+V^{\gamma}F_{\gamma},

(1.12)

were
V
represents the tangential component as mentioned above.

Differentiating (
1.12
) with respect to the spatial variables and using
(
1.5
), together with
m\perp F_{\alpha}
, lead to the evolution
of the moving frame:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}F_{\alpha}=&\,-\operatorname{Im}\left[\left(\partial_{\alpha}^{A}\psi-\sqrt{-1}\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}V^{\gamma}\right)\overline{m}\right]\\
&+\left[\operatorname{Im}\left(\psi\overline{\lambda}_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\right)+\nabla_{\alpha}V^{\gamma}\right]F_{\gamma},\\
\partial_{t}^{B}m=&\,-\sqrt{-1}\left(\partial^{A,\alpha}\psi-\sqrt{-1}\lambda_{\gamma}^{\alpha}V^{\gamma}\right)F_{\alpha},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.13)

where

\partial_{t}^{B}:=\partial_{t}+\sqrt{-1}\,B.

The spatial and temporal covariant derivatives satisfy

\left[\partial_{t}^{B},\partial_{\alpha}^{A}\right]m=\sqrt{-1}\left(\partial_{t}A_{\alpha}-\partial_{\alpha}B\right)m.

Using (
1.5
) and (
1.13
) and equating the tangential
and normal components, respectively, we obtain the evolution equation for
the complex second fundamental form,

\partial_{t}^{B}\lambda^{\sigma}_{\alpha}+\lambda^{\gamma}_{\alpha}\left(\operatorname{Im}(\psi\bar{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{\gamma})+\nabla_{\gamma}V^{\sigma}\right)=\sqrt{-1}\nabla_{\alpha}^{A}\left(\partial^{A,\sigma}\psi-\sqrt{-1}\lambda_{\gamma}^{\sigma}V^{\gamma}\right),

(1.14)

as well as the compatibility condition

\partial_{t}A_{\alpha}-\partial_{\alpha}B=\operatorname{Re}\left(\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\bar{\partial}_{\gamma}^{A}\overline{\psi}\right)-\operatorname{Im}\left(\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\bar{\lambda}_{\gamma\sigma}\right)V^{\sigma},

(1.15)

where
\overline{\partial}_{\gamma}^{A}=\partial_{\gamma}-\sqrt{-1}A_{\gamma}
.

Under the Coulomb gauge condition (
1.4
), the evolution equation (
1.14
) further reduces to the Schrödinger type equation for the complex scalar mean curvature
\psi

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sqrt{-1}\partial_{t}\psi+g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\psi=\sqrt{-1}(V-2A)_{\alpha}\nabla^{\alpha}\psi-\sqrt{-1}\lambda_{\sigma}^{\gamma}\operatorname{Im}\left(\psi\bar{\lambda}_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\right)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\left(B+A_{\alpha}A^{\alpha}-V_{\alpha}A^{\alpha}\right)\psi,\\
\psi(0)=\psi_{0},\end{cases}

where
g^{\alpha\beta}=h^{\alpha\beta}+\delta_{\alpha\beta}
,
\lambda
is complex-valued, whereas
g,V,A
, and
B
are
real-valued.
And the above equation is equivalent to the following divergence form

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sqrt{-1}\partial_{t}\psi+\partial_{\alpha}(g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\psi)=\mathcal{N},\\
\psi(0)=\psi_{0},\end{cases}

(1.16)

with the nonlinear term

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}:=

\displaystyle\,\sqrt{-1}(V-2A)_{\alpha}\nabla^{\alpha}\psi-\sqrt{-1}\lambda_{\sigma}^{\gamma}\operatorname{Im}\left(\psi\bar{\lambda}_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(B+A_{\alpha}A^{\alpha}-V_{\alpha}A^{\alpha}\right)\psi+\partial_{\alpha}h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\psi.

It remains to determine the auxiliary variables

(\lambda,g,V,A,B)

at each fixed time. The first equation below is precisely the div-curl
system obtained from the Codazzi identities
(
1.7
) and
(
1.10
). The metric equation follows from the Gauss
equation in harmonic coordinates, the
A
-equation follows from the
Ricci equation and the Coulomb condition, while the
V
-equation is chosen
so that the harmonic coordinate condition is propagated by the flow.
Altogether, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\nabla_{\alpha}^{A}\lambda_{\beta\gamma}-\nabla_{\beta}^{A}\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}=0,\quad\nabla_{\beta}^{A}\psi=\nabla^{A,\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha\beta}=\nabla_{\alpha}^{A}\lambda^{\alpha}_{\beta},\\
&g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}g_{\gamma\sigma}={\left[-\partial_{\gamma}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}g_{\alpha\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}g_{\alpha\gamma}+\partial_{\gamma}g_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\sigma}g^{\alpha\beta}\right]}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,+2g^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{\sigma\alpha,\nu}\Gamma_{\beta\gamma}^{\nu}-2\operatorname{Re}\left(\lambda_{\gamma\sigma}\overline{\psi}-\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma}^{\alpha}\right),\\
&\nabla^{\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}V^{\gamma}=-2\nabla_{\alpha}\operatorname{Im}\left(\psi\bar{\lambda}^{\alpha\gamma}\right)-\operatorname{Re}\left(\lambda_{\sigma}^{\gamma}\overline{\psi}-\lambda_{\alpha\sigma}\bar{\lambda}^{\alpha\gamma}\right)V^{\sigma}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,+2\big(\operatorname{Im}(\psi\bar{\lambda}^{\alpha\beta})+\nabla^{\alpha}V^{\beta}\big)\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma},\\
&\nabla^{\gamma}\nabla_{\gamma}A_{\alpha}=\operatorname{Re}(\psi\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha}^{\sigma}-\lambda_{\alpha}^{\beta}\bar{\lambda}_{\beta}^{\sigma})A_{\sigma}+\nabla^{\gamma}\operatorname{Im}\left(\lambda_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha\sigma}\right).\end{aligned}\right.

(1.17)

Applying
\nabla^{\alpha}
to (
1.15
) and using the gauge conditions
give the elliptic equation for
B
:

\displaystyle\nabla^{\gamma}\nabla_{\gamma}B=

\displaystyle\,-\nabla^{\gamma}\left[\operatorname{Re}\left(\lambda_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\bar{\partial}^{A}_{\sigma}\bar{\psi}\right)-\operatorname{Im}\left(\lambda_{\gamma}^{\sigma}\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma\beta}\right)V^{\beta}\right]

\displaystyle+\left(2\operatorname{Im}\left(\psi\bar{\lambda}^{\beta\gamma}\right)+\nabla^{\beta}V^{\gamma}+\nabla^{\gamma}V^{\beta}\right)\partial_{\beta}A_{\gamma}.

(1.18)

The metric evolves according to

\partial_{t}g_{\alpha\beta}=2\operatorname{Im}(\psi\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta})+\nabla_{\alpha}V_{\beta}+\nabla_{\beta}V_{\alpha}.

(1.19)

Finally, we fix the remaining gauge freedom by imposing

\displaystyle\lambda(\infty)=0,\quad g(\infty)=I_{d},\quad V(\infty)=0,\quad A(\infty)=0,\quad B(\infty)=0

(1.20)

in the appropriate averaged sense.

For later estimates it is convenient to use

u_{1}=\psi,\qquad u_{2}=\lambda,\qquad u_{3}=\nabla h,\qquad u_{4}=V,\qquad u_{5}=A.

These five variables have compatible scaling and can be treated in a unified
multilinear framework, while
B
admits a different scaling and will be estimated separately.

1.4.
Balance laws, bilinear estimate and interaction Morawetz estimate

The main difficulty is not the linear Schrödinger evolution but the
frequency interactions generated by the quasilinear metric and the elliptic
variables. A direct use of Strichartz and energy estimates is insufficient at the
critical endpoint: low–high interactions may lose derivatives, while
high-high interactions may generate dangerous low frequencies. Our proof
exploits two complementary physical space mechanisms.

For the linear model

\sqrt{-1}\partial_{t}u+\Delta u=0,

one has the mass and momentum balance laws. After fixing a direction, say

x_{\gamma}
, and integrating over the transverse variables

\widehat{x}_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, these identities become a pair of one-dimensional
balance laws in
(t,x_{\gamma})
. Then applying a new
div-curl
lemma introduced by Zhou
[
47
]

to the mass density of a low-frequency wave and the directional momentum density
of a high-frequency wave yields, for
\mu\ll\rho
, the model estimate

\sup_{\tau}\left\|\|P_{\mu}u\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\|P_{\rho,\gamma}u^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{\gamma}}}\lesssim\mu^{-s}c_{\mu}(0)\,\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma).

(1.21)

The gain
\rho^{-1/2}
is the decisive feature, which is a genuinely bilinear
spacetime gain and cannot be recovered from energy estimates alone. Moreover,
the directional decomposition
P_{\rho,\gamma}=P_{\rho}\Xi_{\gamma}(D)
allows one to
trade the high-frequency factor for a derivative in the distinguished direction.
Consequently (
1.21
) is especially effective for the low–high and
high–low paraproducts that arise in the elliptic equations and commutators.

We should mention that the new
div-curl
lemma has revealed great potential of application to the regularity problem of dispersive equations, such as extremal hypersurface equation
[
41
]
, Schrödinger map flow
[
42
]
, wave equation
[
43
]
, wave map
[
24
]
, Schrödinger equation
[
31
]
, mKdV/modified Benjamin-Ono equation
[
40
]
and the Zakharov system
[
11
]
.

For the quasilinear equation the exact conservation laws acquire the source terms
coming from
\mathcal{N}
, the metric perturbation
h
, and
\partial_{t}g
. A major
part of the proof consists in showing that the geometric structure makes these
errors perturbative under the bootstrap assumptions. Thus the balance laws are
not merely formal identities: they provide the spacetime mechanism that recovers
the half derivative needed to close the critical frequency summations.

The bilinear estimate mentioned above is directional and is naturally tied to separated
frequencies, which is therefore not appropriate for the high-high
interaction terms. To treat those terms we adapt the interaction Morawetz estimate of Colliander, Keel, Staffilani, Takaoka and Tao
[
3
]
for semilinear Schrödinger equation to the present quasilinear system. Splitting

x=(y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-3}
and using the interaction potential associated with

a(y)=|y|
, the mass and momentum balance laws yield the coercive term

-\Delta_{y}^{2}|y|=8\pi\delta_{0},

and hence the interaction Morawetz type estimate

\|P_{\rho}u\|_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{2}_{z}}\lesssim\rho^{-s+\frac{1}{4}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma).

(1.22)

This estimate is translation invariant, does not require the directional
multiplier
\Xi_{\gamma}(D)
, and is therefore well suited to comparable-frequency
and high–high interactions. The
L^{4}
spacetime gain supplies precisely the
integrability needed to control quartic expressions generated by the interaction
of two quadratic geometric terms. Interaction Morawetz estimates are some kind of average in space of classical Morawetz estimates (see
[
35
,
2
]
) and work well for the nonlinear Schrödinger equation with general initial data. The first example of this kind of estimate was proved in the famous work
[
3
]
for Schrödinger equation in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and then has been developed for other dimensional cases (see
[
4
,
30
,
45
,
46
]
and references therein).

In conclusion, the two estimates are complementary as follows:

\boxed{\text{new div-curl lemma}\Longrightarrow\text{ bilinear estimate}\Longrightarrow\text{low-high/high-low control}},

whereas

\boxed{\text{interaction Morawetz estimate}\Longrightarrow\text{high-high/comparable-frequency control}}.

Together with the energy and elliptic estimates, they replace the derivative room
that would otherwise have to be supplied by working above scaling.

1.5.
Structural features used in the proof

We should mention that some geometric structures are also indispensable to obtain our main result.

First, the Codazzi and contracted Codazzi identities form a covariant div-curl system for the complex second fundamental form
\lambda
. Through the Hodge identity, this yields an elliptic reconstruction of the schematic form

\lambda=\nabla\Delta^{-1}\nabla\psi+\nabla\Delta^{-1}(\text{quadratic geometric terms}),

so that the principal map from
\psi
to
\lambda
is of order zero. In particular, derivatives occurring in the nonlinear source terms can be traded through the div–curl structure and compensated by the elliptic inverse derivative, rather than accumulating on a single high-frequency factor.

Second, the Gauss equation forces the quadratic curvature contribution to the metric equation to appear in the special combination

(\lambda\wedge\lambda)_{\gamma\sigma}=\lambda_{\gamma\sigma}\overline{\psi}-\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}\overline{\lambda^{\alpha}{}_{\sigma}}.

Combined with the Codazzi representation of
\lambda
, this tensorial structure exhibits a cancellation when two comparable high frequencies interact to produce a much lower output frequency. This gain is essential in dimension five, as it compensates for the low-frequency singularity of the inverse elliptic operator in the reconstruction of the metric, thereby allowing the high–high-to-low interactions arising in the weak difference estimates to be summed at the critical regularity.

Third, the metric evolution has a favorable structure for the interaction Morawetz identity in the quasilinear setting: the time derivative of the principal coefficient is given schematically by

\partial_{t}g=\underbrace{\nabla V}_{differentiated~elliptic}+\underbrace{\lambda\psi}_{quadratic~curvature}+\text{lower-order terms}.

Thus the coefficient variation term arising in the momentum balance law is either differentiated elliptic or quadratic in the curvature variables, and is therefore perturbative in the critical bootstrap regime.

1.6.
Main results

We first state global well-posedness for the Schrödinger–elliptic
system.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
d\geq 5
and
s=s_{c}=\frac{d}{2}-1
. There exists
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small such that, whenever

\|\psi_{0}\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\varepsilon,

(1.23)

the Schrödinger equation (
1.16
), coupled with the elliptic system (
1.17
) and (
1.18
), is globally well-posed in

\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The resulting solution also satisfies the corresponding
geometric time-evolution equations (
1.14
), (
1.15
) and (
1.19
).

Turning back to the geometric flow gives the following result.

Corollary 1.2

(Global regularity for SMCF)
.

Let
d\geq 5
and
s=\frac{d}{2}-1
. If the initial submanifold is a sufficiently small
perturbation of a Euclidean plane and

\|\nabla(g_{0}-\mathrm{Id})\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|H_{0}\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\varepsilon,

then the SMCF (
1.2
) for maps from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into
\mathbb{R}^{d+2}
admits a
global solution in the harmonic coordinates and Coulomb gauge.

Theorem
1.1
will be proved by using bootstrap argument. Starting
from the local solution in the harmonic coordinate and Coulomb gauge, we first assume enlarged versions of the
critical energy, local-smoothing/elliptic, bilinear and interaction Morawetz bounds.
The nonlinear estimates, together with the geometric elliptic equations then improve
the bootstrap constants, and hence the global regularity follows.

To formulate the bootstrap assumptions, we use the
\sigma
-frequency envelope introduced in Tao
[
36
]
:

\displaystyle\|P_{\rho}\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\rho^{-s-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(1.24)

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho}c_{\rho}(\sigma)\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|\psi_{0}\|_{\dot{B}^{s+\sigma}_{2,1}},~~\sigma=0,2,

(1.25)

\displaystyle c_{\nu}(\sigma)\lesssim

\displaystyle~c_{\rho}(\sigma)2^{\delta|l-k|},

(1.26)

where
\nu=2^{l},\rho=2^{k},
and
c_{\rho}(\sigma)
is a constant depending on
\rho
for
\sigma=0,2.
We shall refer to the condition (
1.26
)
as the slow variation property of the
\sigma
-frequency envelope. In particular,
for
\nu\gtrsim\rho,
this property gives

\rho^{\delta}c_{\nu}(\sigma)\lesssim\rho^{\delta}c_{\rho}(\sigma)2^{\delta(l-k)}\lesssim\nu^{\delta}c_{\rho}(\sigma).

The case
\sigma=0
tracks the critical norm, while
\sigma=2
propagates two
additional derivatives for smooth approximations and higher-regularity persistence.

Using the notations of Subsection
2.1
, we impose the
following bootstrap assumptions on
u_{i}
and
B
:

\displaystyle\|P_{\rho}u_{i}(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\rho^{-s-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(1.27)

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\rho}u_{\mathfrak{T}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\rho^{-s-1-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(1.28)

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau}

\displaystyle\,\big\|\|P_{\mu}u_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\ \|P_{\rho,\gamma}u_{j}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{\gamma}}}

(1.29)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}\mu^{-s}c_{\mu}(0)\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),~\text{ for }\mu\ll\rho,

\displaystyle\sup_{J}

\displaystyle\,\|P_{\rho}u_{i}\|_{L^{4}_{t,y_{J}}L^{2}_{z_{J}}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\rho^{-s+\frac{1}{4}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(1.30)

\displaystyle\left\|B_{\rho}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\rho^{-s-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(1.31)

where
\varepsilon
is given by (
1.23
),
P_{\rho,\gamma}=P_{\rho}\Xi_{\gamma}(D)
with
\gamma=1,\dots,d
, and

\mathfrak{T}\in\{3,4,5\},\quad i,j\in\{1,2,3,4,5\},\quad\sigma\in\{0,2\}.

Let
\mathfrak{I}_{3}:=\left\{J=\{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,d\}:j_{1}<j_{2}<j_{3}\right\},

then for each
J\in\mathfrak{I}_{3}
, write

x=(y_{J},z_{J})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-3}
, the norm in (
1.30
) means

\|\cdot\|_{L^{4}_{t,y_{J}}L^{2}_{z_{J}}}:=\big\|\|\cdot\|_{L_{z_{J}}^{2}}\big\|_{L_{t,y_{J}}^{4}}.

Noting that the norms in the left hand side of (
1.29
) and (
1.30
) are translation invariant.

Based on the bootstrap assumptions (
1.27
)–(
1.31
), we can prove
Theorem
1.1
by establishing the following a priori estimates for the Schrödinger–elliptic system (
1.16
)–(
1.18
)

\displaystyle\|P_{\rho}u_{i}(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\rho^{-s-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(1.32)

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\rho}u_{\mathfrak{T}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim\rho^{-s-1-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma)

(1.33)

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau}

\displaystyle\big\|\|P_{\mu}u_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\|P_{\rho,\gamma}u_{j}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{\gamma}}}

(1.34)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\mu^{-s}c_{\mu}(0)\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),~\text{ for }\mu\ll\rho,

\displaystyle\sup_{J}

\displaystyle\ \|P_{\rho}u_{i}\|_{L^{4}_{t,y_{J}}L^{2}_{z_{J}}}\lesssim\rho^{-s+\frac{1}{4}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(1.35)

\displaystyle\left\|B_{\rho}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim\rho^{-s-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(1.36)

with the same notations as in (
1.27
)–(
1.31
), which may further be stated as the following theorem.

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose
d\geq 5,s=\frac{d}{2}-1
and there exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small such that the initial data
\psi_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\psi_{0}\|_{\dot{B}_{2,1}^{s}}\leq\varepsilon.

Then the critical inequalities (
1.32
)–(
1.36
), corresponding
to
\sigma=0
, hold for all
t>0
, where

u_{1}=\psi
,
u_{2}=\lambda
,
u_{3}=\nabla h
,
u_{4}=V
, and

u_{5}=A
are solutions of the Schrödinger–elliptic system
(
1.16
)–(
1.18
). If, in addition, the initial
data admit a frequency envelope
c_{\rho}(2)
satisfying
(
1.24
)–(
1.26
) with
\sigma=2
, then the same estimates
also hold with
\sigma=2
.

The proof proceeds in five mutually reinforcing stages: nonlinear paraproduct and
commutator estimates; energy estimates for the Schrödinger component; the
mass–momentum/div–curl bilinear estimate; the quasilinear interaction Morawetz
estimate; and elliptic estimates for
\lambda,h,V,A,B
. The resulting bounds
strictly improve the bootstrap assumptions, after which the standard continuity
argument gives global control and the usual approximation/difference argument
yields existence, uniqueness and continuous dependence at the critical Besov
regularity.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notations

Greek indices

\alpha,\beta,\gamma
range over
\{1,\ldots,d\}
and are summed when repeated.
For
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
, let

\widehat{x_{\gamma}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\gamma-1},x_{\gamma+1},\ldots,x_{d}).

We write
|\nabla|=(-\Delta)^{1/2}
and

u^{\tau}(x)=u(x-\tau)
.
\partial_{x}
denotes an arbitrary first‑order partial derivative
\partial_{\alpha}
with
\alpha=1,\dots,d
, while

\partial_{x}^{2}
denotes an arbitrary second‑order partial derivative
\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}
.

Let the spatial Fourier transform
\hat{\phi}(t,\xi)
be defined by

\mathscr{F}\phi=\hat{\phi}(t,\xi):=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\xi}\phi(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x,

with the inverse transform given by

\mathscr{F}^{-1}u=\check{u}(t,x):=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}e^{2\pi ix\cdot\xi}u(t,\xi)\,d\xi.

Next, we introduce the following decomposition which corresponds to decomposing the frequency space according to direction. As in the dyadic partition of unity for the Littlewood–Paley decomposition, there exists
\zeta_{\gamma}(\xi)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
supported in
|\xi|\lesssim|\xi_{\gamma}|
such that

\sum_{\gamma=1}^{d}\zeta_{\gamma}(\xi)=1,\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

and

\text{Id}=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{d}\Xi_{\gamma}(D).

(2.1)

Let
\chi(\xi)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a smooth function supported in
|\xi|\leq 2
and equal to
1
on
|\xi|\leq 1
. The Littlewood–Paley operators
P_{\rho}
for
\rho\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
are defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{P_{\leq\rho}u}(\xi):=q\left(\rho^{-1}\xi\right)\widehat{u}(\xi),\quad P_{\rho}=P_{\leq\rho}-P_{\leq\rho/2}

with symbol
\chi_{\rho}(\xi)=\chi_{1}(\rho^{-1}\xi)
, where
q_{1}(\xi)
supported in
\{1/2\leq|\xi|\leq 2\}
is given by

\displaystyle\chi_{1}\left(\xi\right)=\chi\left(\xi\right)-\chi\left(2\xi\right).

(2.2)

We denote

\displaystyle P_{\sim\rho}:=\sum_{\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho}P_{\rho^{\prime}},\ P_{\ll\rho}u:=\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}P_{\mu}u,\,

\displaystyle P_{\rho,\gamma}:=P_{\rho}\Xi_{\gamma}(D),\ P_{\sim\rho,\gamma}:=\sum_{\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho}P_{\rho^{\prime},\gamma},

where
\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho=|\log\rho^{\prime}-\log\rho|\leq 4
,
and use the short hand

\displaystyle u_{\ll\rho}:=P_{\ll\rho}u,\ v_{\rho,\gamma}:=P_{\rho,\gamma}v.

We will also use Greek indices
\mu,\nu,\mu^{\prime},\dots
to denote the Littlewood–Paley projectors

P_{\mu},P_{\nu},P_{\mu^{\prime}}\ \text{etc.}

where
\mu,\nu,\mu^{\prime},\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

The standard Bony’s decomposition reads as

\displaystyle P_{\rho}(u_{i}u_{j})=

\displaystyle\,P_{\lesssim\rho}u_{i}P_{\sim\rho}u_{j}+P_{\lesssim\rho}u_{j}P_{\sim\rho}u_{i}+P_{\rho}\Big(\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\gtrsim\rho}P_{\nu}u_{i}P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j}\Big),

(2.3)

where the first term on the right-hand side is referred to as the
low–high
term, the second term as the
high–low
term, and the third term as the
high–high
term.

With a slight abuse of notation, sometimes we denote

\displaystyle P_{\rho}(u_{i}u_{j})=P_{\ll\rho}u_{i}P_{\rho}u_{j}+P_{\ll\rho}u_{j}P_{\rho}u_{i}+P_{\rho}\Big(\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\gtrsim\rho}P_{\nu}u_{i}P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j}\Big),

(2.4)

as the finite sum of
P_{\sim\rho}=\sum_{\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho}P_{\rho^{\prime}}
would not make a difference in this paper.

Let
f^{*}
denote the decreasing rearrangement of
f
. The Lorentz
space
L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is equipped with the quasi-norm

\|f\|_{L^{p,q}}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(t^{1/p}f^{*}(t)\big)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{1/q},&1\leq q<\infty,\\[5.16663pt]
\displaystyle\sup_{t>0}t^{1/p}f^{*}(t),&q=\infty.\end{cases}

In dimension three, we shall use the Lorentz–Hölder inequality

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|f(y)g(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|g\|_{L^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},

as well as the Lorentz–Sobolev and endpoint
Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequalities

\big\||\nabla_{y}|^{-1/2}f\big\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\lesssim\|\nabla_{y}f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},\quad\big\||\nabla_{y}|^{-3/2}g\big\|_{L^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

in Section
6
.

2.2.
Multilinear expressions

Following
[
20
,
37
]
, we introduce the
L
notation for multilinear expressions.
Given scalar functions
(\phi_{i})_{1\leq i\leq S}
, we denote by
L(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{S})
any multilinear expression of the form

\displaystyle L(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{S})(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\int K(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{S})\,\phi_{1}^{\tau_{1}}(t,x)\cdots\phi_{S}^{\tau_{S}}(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}y_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}y,

where we denote by
\phi^{\tau}(x)=\phi(x-\tau)
the translation and
K
is a measure with bounded mass. The exact value of
K
may change from line to line, and we require their mass are uniformly bounded by a sufficiently large constant . The kernel
K
is never allowed to depend on the index
\alpha
.
We also extend this notation to the case where
(\phi_{i})_{1\leq i\leq S}
take values as
d
dimensional vectors or
d\times d
matrices by allowing
K
to be an appropriate tensor.

We introduce the
L
notation for two reasons. First, all nonlinear terms in the sequel can be expressed uniformly in this notation. Second, the
L
notation streamlines our multilinear estimates by allowing us to consider only the case where each term is translated — a reduction that is justified by the translation invariance of the spaces on both sides of the estimate. For example, if we have the bounds with translation invariant norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
and
\|\cdot\|_{X_{i}}
for
i=1,2,3
:

\sup_{\tau_{\alpha}}\|u_{1}u_{2}^{\tau_{2}}u_{3}^{\tau_{3}}\|_{X}\leq C\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|u_{i}\|_{X_{i}},

then for any trilinear form
L
with integrable kernel we have

\displaystyle\|L(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\tau_{\alpha}}\int K(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3})\|u_{1}^{\tau_{1}}u_{2}^{\tau_{2}}u_{3}^{\tau_{3}}\|_{X}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tau_{\alpha}}\int K(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3})\|u_{1}u_{2}^{\tau_{2}}u_{3}^{\tau_{3}}\|_{X}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim C\prod_{i=1}^{3}\|u_{i}\|_{X_{i}}.

For more details of
L
notation, we refer to
[
20
,
37
]
.

We next list some basic properties of the
L
notation. The
L
notation interacts well with Littlewood–Paley operators:

\displaystyle L(\partial_{x}P_{\leq\rho}\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{S})=\rho L(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{S}),

(2.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}P_{\leq\rho}L(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{S})=\rho L(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{S}),.

(2.6)

The
L
notation is also useful as it enables the conversion between derivatives and multipliers, a point we will revisit frequently in the bilinear estimates. Since
P_{\rho}
are homogeneous Littlewood-Paley operators, we have

\displaystyle L(P_{\rho}\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{S})=\rho^{-1}L(\partial_{x}P_{\rho}\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{S}).

(2.7)

Moreover, we can rewrite
P_{\rho,\gamma}v
as

\displaystyle P_{\rho,\gamma}v=\rho^{-1}\widecheck{\chi_{\rho,\gamma}}*(\partial_{\gamma}P_{\rho,\gamma}v),

where

\chi_{\rho,\gamma}(\xi)=-\sqrt{-1}\zeta_{\alpha}\big(\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\big)\chi_{1}\big(\frac{\xi}{\rho}\big)\big(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\rho}\big)^{-1}.

(2.8)

Using the fact that
\Xi_{\gamma}(D)
are homogeneous operators and
P_{\rho,\gamma}=P_{N}\Xi_{\gamma}(D)
has Fourier support in
\{|\xi|\lesssim|\xi_{\gamma}|\}
, we conclude that
\chi_{\rho,\gamma}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and hence

\displaystyle L(P_{\rho,\gamma}\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{S})=\rho^{-1}L(\partial_{\gamma}P_{\rho,\gamma}\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{S}).

(2.9)

Below we give an example of the application of (
2.9
) that is most frequently used in the bilinear estimates in the sequel:

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau}\left\|\|u_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\|P_{\rho,\gamma}v^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{\gamma}}}

(2.10)

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{-1}\sup_{\tau}\left\|\|u_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\|\partial_{\gamma}P_{\rho,\gamma}v^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{\gamma}}}.

2.3.
Commutator estimates

Another setting in which the

L
notation is especially convenient arises in commutator expressions, namely

Lemma 2.1

(Leibniz rule for
P_{\rho}
and
P_{\rho,\alpha}
,
[
37
]
)
.

We have the commutator identity

Q_{\rho}(fg)=f\cdot Q_{\rho}g+\rho^{-1}L\left(\partial_{x}f,g\right),\ Q_{\rho}=P_{\rho},P_{\rho,\alpha}.

Proof.

Since
P_{\rho,\alpha}=P_{\rho}\Xi_{\alpha}(D)
where
\Xi_{\alpha}(D)
are zero-th order homogeneous operators, the symbols of
P_{\rho}
and
P_{\rho,\alpha}
take the form of
\chi(\rho^{-1}\xi)
. Direct computation shows that

\displaystyle[Q_{\rho},f]g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{d}\widecheck{\chi}(\rho y)(f(x-y)-f(x))g(x-y)dy

\displaystyle=-\rho^{-1}\int^{1}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho^{d}(\rho y)\widecheck{\chi}(\rho y)\cdot\partial_{x}f(x-sy)g(x-y)dyds.

The conclusion follows from the rapid decay of
\widecheck{\chi}
.
∎

Applying Lemma
2.1
and the Bony’s decomposition (
2.3
), we obtain for
Q_{\rho}=P_{\rho}
or
P_{\rho,\alpha}
the following commutator estimate:

\displaystyle[Q_{\rho},f]g=Q_{\rho}(fg)-fQ_{\rho}g

(2.11)

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}L(\partial_{x}P_{\ll\rho}f,P_{\sim\rho}g)+L(P_{\ll\rho}g,P_{\sim\rho}f)+\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}P_{\rho}L(P_{\nu}f,P_{\nu^{\prime}}g),

where Lemma
2.1
is used for the terms containing low-frequency components of
f
.

2.4.
New type div-curl lemma

The following
div-curl
lemma, which was first introduced by the fourth author
[
47
]
, plays a crucial role in our proof.

Lemma 2.2

(div-curl Lemma)
.

Suppose that
f^{ij},i,j=1,2
satisfy

\begin{gathered}\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{11}+\partial_{x}f^{12}=G^{1},\\
\partial_{t}f^{21}-\partial_{x}f^{22}=G^{2},\\
\end{cases}\\
f^{11},f^{12},f^{21},f^{22}\rightarrow 0,\text{ as }x\rightarrow\infty,\end{gathered}

then it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}f^{11}f^{22}+f^{12}f^{21}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(2.12)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\max_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}\left\|f^{11}(t)\right\|_{L^{1}}\cdot\max_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}\left\|f^{21}(t)\right\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle+\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{x}f^{11}(t,y)\mathrm{d}y\right)G^{2}(t,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(\int^{+\infty}_{x}f^{21}(t,y)\mathrm{d}y\right)G^{1}(t,x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\right|,

provided that the right side is bounded.

Proof.

The same computation as in
[
42
]
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\int_{x<y}f^{11}(t,x)f^{21}(t,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(f^{11}f^{22}+f^{12}f^{21}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{x}f^{11}(t,y)\mathrm{d}y\right)G^{2}(t,x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(\int^{+\infty}_{x}f^{21}(t,y)\mathrm{d}y\right)G^{1}(t,x)\mathrm{d}x,

then (
2.12
) follows by integrating the above inequality with respect to
t
over
[0,T]
.
∎

2.5.
Estimates for the linear Schrödinger equation

To motivate our estimate for the nonlinear equations, we first demonstrate the corresponding estimates for the linear Schrödinger equation to help the readers to better understand our proof, thus, we first use the assumption (
1.27
)–(
1.30
) to obtain the estimates (
1.32
)–(
1.35
) for

\begin{cases}\sqrt{-1}\partial_{t}u+\Delta u=0,\\
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}.\end{cases}

(2.13)

Lemma 2.3

(Energy estimates)
.

If the initial data of (
2.13
) satisfies (
1.24
), then (
1.32
) holds for the solution of (
2.13
).

Proof.

Applying the operator

P_{\rho}
to (
2.13
), we get

\displaystyle\sqrt{-1}\partial_{t}u_{\rho}+\Delta u_{\rho}=0,

(2.14)

from which the mass conservation law

\|u_{\rho}\|_{L^{2}}=\|P_{\rho}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}

follows directly, and hence yields (
1.32
) by combining (
1.24
).
∎

Lemma 2.4

(Bilinear estimate)
.

If
\mu\ll\rho
, and the solution of (
2.13
) satisfies (
1.27
)–(
1.30
), then (
1.34
) also holds, i.e.

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau}\left\|\|u_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\|P_{\rho,\gamma}u^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{\gamma}}}

(2.15)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\mu^{-s}c_{\mu}(0)\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),~~\sigma=0,2.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
\gamma=1
, and define
\phi_{\rho}=P_{\rho,1}u^{\tau}
.

Employing
P_{\mu}
and
\Xi_{1}(D)P_{\rho}
to the equation (
2.13
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sqrt{-1}\partial_{t}u_{\mu}+\Delta u_{\mu}=0,

(2.16)

\displaystyle\sqrt{-1}\partial_{t}\phi_{\rho}+\Delta\phi_{\rho}=0

(2.17)

respectively. It is easy to get the balance law of mass to (
2.16
) and the balance law of momentum to (
2.17
) as follows

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}|u_{\mu}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}-\partial_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\mathrm{Im}(u_{\mu}\partial_{1}\overline{u}_{\mu})\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\mathrm{Im}(\phi_{\rho}\partial_{1}\overline{\phi}_{\rho})\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}-\partial_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}2|\partial_{1}\phi_{\rho}|^{2}-\partial_{1}^{2}\frac{|\phi_{\rho}|^{2}}{2}\ \mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}=0.

Applying the div-curl Lemma
2.2
to the above balance laws and by (
2.9
), we can integrate by parts to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sup_{\tau}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}|u_{\mu}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}|\partial_{1}\phi_{\rho}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}t\\
&+\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\mathrm{Re}(u_{\mu}\partial_{1}\overline{u}_{\mu})\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\mathrm{Re}(\phi_{\rho}\partial_{1}\overline{\phi}_{\rho})\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}t\\
&-\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\mathrm{Im}(u_{\mu}\partial_{1}\overline{u}_{\mu})\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\mathrm{Im}(\phi_{\rho}\partial_{1}\overline{\phi}_{\rho})\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}t\\
:=&\,\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}+\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}+\widetilde{\mathrm{III}}\\
\lesssim&\,\rho\cdot\max_{0\leq s\leq t}\|u_{\mu}(s)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\|\phi_{\rho}(s)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}.\end{aligned}

(2.18)

It follows from (
2.9
) and
\mu\ll\rho
that

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}+\widetilde{\mathrm{III}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}\|u_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|\partial_{1}u_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|\phi_{\rho}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|\partial_{1}\phi_{\rho}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\mu\rho^{-1}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}\|u_{\mu}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|\partial_{1}\phi_{\rho}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\,\widetilde{\mathrm{I}},

which gives the desired estimates since

\displaystyle\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}|u_{\mu}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}|\phi_{\rho}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\widehat{x}_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\rho^{-2}\ \widetilde{\mathrm{I}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\rho^{-1}\cdot\max_{0\leq s\leq t}\|u_{\mu}(s)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\|\phi_{\rho}(s)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\mu^{-2s}c_{\mu}^{2}(0)\rho^{-2s-1-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma).

∎

We next introduce the interaction Morawetz estimate (
1.35
) for (
2.13
).

Lemma 2.5

(Interaction Morawetz estimate)
.

If the initial data of (
2.13
) satisfies (
1.24
), then for the solution of (
2.13
), we have
(
1.35
).

Proof.

Applying the operator

P_{\rho}
to the equation (
2.13
), we get a linear equation as (
2.14
). Thus
the same manner as in Appendix
A
yields

\displaystyle\|u_{\rho}\|^{4}_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{2}_{z}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\|u_{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}^{3}\|\partial_{x}u_{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\rho^{-4s+1-4\sigma}c^{4}_{\rho}(\sigma),

(2.19)

which verifies (
1.35
).
∎

2.6.
The general form for the equations and nonlinear terms

2.6.1.
Elliptic equations for
\lambda,h,A

As in
[
13
,
14
]
, we will study the following simplified elliptic equations instead of (
1.17
) for convenience

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{\beta}\psi+A\cdot\psi+h\cdot\partial_{x}\lambda+\partial_{x}h\cdot\lambda,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\lambda_{\beta\gamma}-\partial_{\beta}\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}=A\cdot\lambda+\partial_{x}h\cdot\lambda,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\Delta h=h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}h+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}h+\lambda\wedge\lambda+h\cdot\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}h,

(2.22)

\displaystyle\Delta V=h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}V+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}V

(2.23)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+(\partial_{x}h)^{2}\cdot V+\lambda^{2}(A+V+\partial_{x}h)+\partial_{x}(\lambda^{2}),

\displaystyle\Delta A=h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}A+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}A+(\partial_{x}h)^{2}\cdot A+\lambda^{2}(A+\partial_{x}h)+\partial_{x}(\lambda^{2}),

(2.24)

where
\lambda\wedge\lambda
is defined in (
1.11
). Differentiating (
2.22
) once in the spatial variables and recalling
that
u_{3}=\partial_{x}h
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta u_{3}=h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}u_{3}+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}u_{3}+\partial_{x}(\lambda\wedge\lambda)+u_{3}^{3},

(2.25)

where we suppress the term
h\partial_{x}(u_{3}^{2})
, since Lemma
3.1
gives
\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}}
and thus
1+h\sim 1
.
Noting that (
2.20
) is divergence of
\lambda,
while (
2.21
) is curl of
\lambda,
we then can
use the Hodge decomposition

\Delta U_{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta}\partial_{\beta}^{2}U_{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\left(\partial_{\beta}U_{\alpha}-\partial_{\alpha}U_{\beta}\right)+\sum_{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}U_{\beta}

to get

\displaystyle\Delta\lambda_{\alpha\beta}=\nabla\left(\partial_{\beta}\psi+A\psi+h\partial_{x}\lambda+\partial_{x}h\lambda\right)+\nabla\times\left(A\lambda+\partial_{x}h\lambda\right).

(2.26)

As we have unified the unknowns into
u_{i},\ i=1,2,\cdots,
(
u_{1}=\psi,u_{2}=\lambda,u_{3}=\partial_{x}h,u_{4}=V,u_{5}=A
), the equations (
2.26
), (
2.23
), (
2.24
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Delta u_{2}=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{x}^{2}u_{1}+\partial_{x}(u_{1}\cdot u_{5})+\partial_{x}(h\cdot\partial_{x}u_{2})+\partial_{x}(u_{2}\cdot(u_{3},u_{5})),

(2.27)

\displaystyle\Delta u_{4}=

\displaystyle\,h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}u_{4}+u_{3}\cdot\partial_{x}u_{4}+u_{3}^{2}\cdot u_{4}+u_{2}^{2}(u_{3}+u_{4}+u_{5})+\partial_{x}(u_{2}^{2}),

(2.28)

\displaystyle\Delta u_{5}=

\displaystyle\,h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}u_{5}+u_{3}\cdot\partial_{x}u_{5}+u_{3}^{2}\cdot u_{5}+u_{2}^{2}(u_{3}+u_{5})+\partial_{x}(u_{2}^{2}),

(2.29)

Since
\partial_{x}h,V,A
admit a similar form of elliptic equation and
u_{i}
has the same estimate assumptions (
1.27
)–(
1.30
), we can further simplify system (
2.27
), (
2.25
), (
2.28
) and (
2.29
) into the general forms

\displaystyle\Delta u_{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}^{2}u_{1}+h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}u_{2}+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}u_{2}+\partial_{x}(u_{i}u_{j}),

(2.30)

\displaystyle\Delta u_{\mathfrak{T}}

\displaystyle=h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}u_{\mathfrak{T}}+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}u_{\mathfrak{T}}+\partial_{x}(u_{2}^{2})+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l},

(2.31)

where
\mathfrak{T}\in\{3,4,5\},\ i,j,l\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
.

2.6.2.
Equations for
B

Turning to the unknown function
B
, we can use the second equation in the first line of (
1.17
) and the above rules to rewrite the nonlinear term of (
1.18
) as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\left(\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\bar{\partial}_{\gamma}^{A}\overline{\psi}\right)=

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{Re}\left(\partial_{\gamma}(\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\overline{\psi})\right)-\operatorname{Re}\left(\partial_{\gamma}^{A}\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\cdot\overline{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{Re}\left(\partial_{\gamma}(\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\overline{\psi})\right)-\operatorname{Re}\left(\nabla^{A}_{\gamma}\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\cdot\overline{\psi}\right)+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{Re}\left(\partial_{\gamma}(\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\overline{\psi})\right)-\operatorname{Re}\left(\nabla^{A}_{\alpha}\psi\cdot\overline{\psi}\right)+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{Re}\left(\partial_{\gamma}(\lambda_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\overline{\psi})\right)-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}\left|\psi\right|^{2}+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}.

and consequently to rewrite (
1.18
) as the following schematic form

\displaystyle\Delta B=

\displaystyle\,h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}B+u_{3}\cdot\partial_{x}B+\partial_{x}^{2}(u_{i}u_{j})+\partial_{x}(u_{i}u_{j}u_{l})

\displaystyle+\partial_{x}u_{4}\cdot\partial_{x}u_{5}+u_{i}u_{j}\cdot\partial_{x}u_{l}+u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}u_{l},

(2.32)

where
i,j,k,l\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}.

2.6.3.
Schrödinger equations for
\psi

We first derive a schematic elliptic equation for the combination

W^{\gamma}:=V^{\gamma}-2A^{\gamma},\qquad A^{\gamma}=g^{\gamma\alpha}A_{\alpha}.

Since
\nabla g=0
, raising the index in the elliptic equation for

A_{\alpha}
in (
1.17
) gives

\displaystyle\nabla^{\beta}\nabla_{\beta}A^{\gamma}=

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{Re}\left(\psi\overline{\lambda^{\gamma\sigma}}-\lambda^{\gamma\alpha}\overline{\lambda_{\alpha}^{\sigma}}\right)A_{\sigma}+\nabla_{\alpha}\operatorname{Im}\left(\lambda^{\alpha\sigma}\overline{\lambda^{\gamma}_{\sigma}}\right).

Subtracting twice this equation from the equation for
V^{\gamma}
in
(
1.17
), and using

-\operatorname{Im}\left(\psi\overline{\lambda^{\alpha\gamma}}\right)=\operatorname{Im}\left(\lambda^{\alpha\gamma}\overline{\psi}\right),

we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla^{\beta}\nabla_{\beta}W^{\gamma}=

\displaystyle\,2\nabla_{\alpha}\operatorname{Im}\left(\lambda^{\alpha\gamma}\overline{\psi}-\lambda^{\alpha\sigma}\overline{\lambda^{\gamma}_{\sigma}}\right)+2\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\nabla^{\alpha}V^{\beta}-\operatorname{Re}\left(\lambda^{\gamma}_{\sigma}\overline{\psi}-\lambda_{\alpha\sigma}\overline{\lambda^{\alpha\gamma}}\right)V^{\sigma}

\displaystyle-2\operatorname{Re}\left(\psi\overline{\lambda^{\gamma\sigma}}-\lambda^{\gamma\alpha}\overline{\lambda_{\alpha}^{\sigma}}\right)A_{\sigma}+2\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\operatorname{Im}\left(\psi\overline{\lambda^{\alpha\beta}}\right).

By the definition of the Gauss term in (
1.11
), we have

(\lambda\wedge\lambda)^{\alpha\gamma}=\lambda^{\alpha\gamma}\overline{\psi}-\lambda^{\alpha\sigma}\overline{\lambda^{\gamma}_{\sigma}},

the preceding identity takes the schematic form

\displaystyle\nabla^{\beta}\nabla_{\beta}W^{\gamma}=2\nabla_{\alpha}\operatorname{Im}\left((\lambda\wedge\lambda)^{\alpha\gamma}\right)+2\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\nabla^{\alpha}V^{\beta}+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}.

Expanding the covariant derivatives in Euclidean coordinates and using

g^{\alpha\beta}=\delta^{\alpha\beta}+h^{\alpha\beta},\qquad\Gamma=L(g^{-1},\partial_{x}h),

we obtain schematically

\displaystyle\Delta W=

\displaystyle\,h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}W+\partial_{x}(\lambda\wedge\lambda)+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}V+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}W+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}W+\partial_{x}(\lambda\wedge\lambda)+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}u_{\mathfrak{T}}+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}

(2.33)

with

\mathfrak{T}\in\{3,4,5\}
.

With a slight abuse of notation, we write the nonlinearity

\mathcal{N}
in (
1.16
) in the schematic form

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=

\displaystyle\,(W+u_{3})\cdot\partial_{x}\psi+\psi u_{i}u_{j}+\psi B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathbf{U}\cdot\partial_{x}\psi+\psi u_{i}u_{j}+\psi B,

(2.34)

where
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
,
B
is a real function and
\mathbf{U}:=(W,u_{3})
. It follows from (
2.25
) and
(
2.33
) that
\mathbf{U}
satisfies the schematic
elliptic equation

\displaystyle\Delta\mathbf{U}=h\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}\mathbf{U}+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}u_{\mathfrak{T}}+\partial_{x}(\lambda\wedge\lambda)+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}

(2.35)

for
\mathfrak{T}\in\{3,4,5\}
and
i,j,l\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
.

The term
\psi B
will be treated separately. Indeed,
B
has a different
scaling from the variables
u_{i}
and satisfies a less favorable elliptic
equation, so a direct product estimate for
Q_{\rho}(\psi B)
is not suitable.
Instead, we use the special structure of the Schrödinger identities. In the
energy estimate, taking the imaginary part removes the principal term

BQ_{\rho}\psi
. In the momentum type estimates, taking the real part allows
us to rewrite the contribution of
BQ_{\rho}\psi
as a divergence term plus a
term where the derivative falls on
B
. Thus only the commutator

[Q_{\rho},B]\psi
and differentiated terms involving
\partial_{x}B
need to be
estimated.

Applying the operator
Q_{\rho}=P_{\rho},P_{\rho,\gamma}
to (
1.16
) gives

\sqrt{-1}\partial_{t}\,Q_{\rho}\psi+\partial_{\alpha}(g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\,Q_{\rho}\psi)=Q_{\rho}\mathcal{N}-\partial_{\alpha}([Q_{\rho},h^{\alpha\beta}]\partial_{\beta}\psi),

(2.36)

where

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}\mathcal{N}=

\displaystyle\,Q_{\rho}(\mathbf{U}\cdot\partial_{x}\psi+\psi u_{i}u_{j})+[Q_{\rho},B]\psi+B\cdot Q_{\rho}\psi.

(2.37)

Since
B
is real-valued, the energy identity gives

\operatorname{Im}(Q_{\rho}\overline{\psi}\cdot Q_{\rho}\mathcal{N})=\operatorname{Im}\big(Q_{\rho}\overline{\psi}\cdot\big(Q_{\rho}(\mathbf{U}\cdot\partial_{x}\psi+\psi u_{i}u_{j})+[Q_{\rho},B]\psi\big)\big),

(2.38)

where the main term
B|Q_{\rho}\psi|^{2}
disappears.

For the momentum type identities, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\big(\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}Q_{\rho}\overline{\psi}\cdot Q_{\rho}\mathcal{N}\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Re}\big(\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}Q_{\rho}\overline{\psi}\cdot\big(Q_{\rho}(\mathbf{U}\cdot\partial_{x}\psi+\psi u_{i}u_{j})+[Q_{\rho},B]\psi\big)\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}(B\cdot|Q_{\rho}\psi|^{2})-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}B\cdot|Q_{\rho}\psi|^{2},

(2.39)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}(Q_{\rho}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}Q_{\rho}\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Re}\big(Q_{\rho}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}\big(Q_{\rho}(\mathbf{U}\cdot\partial_{x}\psi+\psi u_{i}u_{j})+[Q_{\rho},B]\psi\big)\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}(B\cdot|Q_{\rho}\psi|^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}B\cdot|Q_{\rho}\psi|^{2}.

(2.40)

Subtracting the two identities, the divergence terms

\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}(B|Q_{\rho}\psi|^{2})
cancel and schematically, we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}Q_{\rho}\overline{\psi}\cdot Q_{\rho}\mathcal{N})-\operatorname{Re}(Q_{\rho}\overline{\psi}\cdot\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}Q_{\rho}\mathcal{N})

(2.41)

\displaystyle=\rho\,L(Q_{\rho}\psi,Q_{\rho}(\mathbf{U}\cdot\partial_{x}\psi+\psi u_{i}u_{j})+[Q_{\rho},B]\psi)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}\big(Q_{\rho}\overline{\psi}\cdot([Q_{\rho},B]\psi)\big)-\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}B\cdot|Q_{\rho}\psi|^{2}.

These identities will be used repeatedly below to avoid estimating

Q_{\rho}(\psi B)
directly.

3.
Nonlinear estimates

This section collects the nonlinear estimates that will be used repeatedly
in the
energy estimate, bilinear estimate, interaction Morawetz estimate
and
elliptic estimate
. Such a summary will help to streamline the analysis in sections
4
-
7
.

The estimates throughout this paper are controlled by the following dyadic summation principle. After applying the relevant estimates and transferring all frequency envelopes to the reference frequency via (
1.26
), it remains only to examine the sign of the residual dyadic power. If the remaining power of a low frequency is positive, then the corresponding low-frequency sum is dominated by its largest admissible frequency, typically the reference frequency
\rho
. If the remaining power of a high frequency is negative, then the high-frequency tail is dominated by its smallest admissible frequency, again typically
\rho
. The small loss introduced by (
1.26
) is harmless, provided that
\delta>0
is chosen sufficiently small.

We first record some direct consequences of the bootstrap assumptions in
Subsection
3.1
, then estimate the typical nonlinear
expressions appearing in the main a priori estimates in Subsection

3.2
.

3.1.
Preliminary estimates from the bootstrap assumptions

We begin with several estimates that follow directly from the bootstrap
assumption. They will be used to place low-frequency factors in
L^{\infty}
-type
norms and to make the dyadic summations converge.

The first lemma gives the basic Besov and
L^{\infty}
controls for the
unknowns
u_{i}
and the low-frequency part of
B
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Assume that (
1.27
) and (
1.31
) hold. Then

\displaystyle\|u_{i}(t)\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}

\displaystyle\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}},

(3.1)

\displaystyle\||\nabla|^{-1}u_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}},

(3.2)

\displaystyle\|P_{\mu}u_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}}\mu,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\|P_{\lesssim\rho}u_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}}\rho,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\|B_{\lesssim\rho}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}}\rho,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}B_{\lesssim\rho}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}L^{\infty}_{z}}

\displaystyle\,\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}}\rho^{\frac{1}{2}},

(3.6)

where
s=\frac{d}{2}-1,i=1,2,3,4,5
.

Proof.

By (
1.25
) and (
1.27
), it is easy to see

\displaystyle\|u_{i}(t)\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho}\rho^{s}\|P_{\rho}u_{i}(t)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\rho}c_{\rho}(0)\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\|\psi_{0}\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}},

which is exactly (
3.1
). Bernstein’s inequality and (
3.1
) then give

\displaystyle\||\nabla|^{-1}u_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\rho}\rho^{-1}\|P_{\rho}u_{i}(t)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\rho}\rho^{s}\|P_{\rho}u_{i}(t)\|_{L_{x}^{2}}=\|u_{i}(t)\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}}.

Estimates (
3.3
)–(
3.6
) follow similarly. For instance, Bernstein’s inequality and (
1.31
) yield

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{x}B_{\lesssim\rho}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}L^{\infty}_{z}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\left\|\partial_{x}B_{\mu}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}L^{\infty}_{z}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\mu^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\mu\left\|B_{\mu}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\mu^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\mu^{-s}c_{\mu}(0)\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}}\rho^{\frac{1}{2}},

which is (
3.6
).
∎

We next record the complementary mixed-norm consequences of the interaction Morawetz bootstrap bounds (
1.30
).

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that (
1.27
), (
1.29
) and (
1.30
) hold. Then

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\nu}\psi\right\|_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{\infty}_{z}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\nu^{-\frac{1}{4}-\sigma}c_{\nu}(\sigma),

(3.7)

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\ll\rho}\partial_{x}\psi\right\|_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{\infty}_{z}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}}\rho^{\frac{3}{4}},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\gtrsim\rho}\left\|L(P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{i},P_{\nu}u_{j})\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim\,\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{4}}\rho^{-s-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(3.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}L(P_{\nu}u_{i},P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|P_{\rho}u_{l}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma),

Proof.

(
3.7
) follows directly from (
1.30
):

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\nu}\psi\right\|_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{\infty}_{z}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\nu^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left\|\psi_{\nu}\right\|_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{2}_{z}}

Similarly, Bernstein’s inequality in the
z
variables and (
1.30
) imply (
3.8
):

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\ll\rho}\partial_{x}\psi\right\|_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{\infty}_{z}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\mu\cdot\mu^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left\|\psi_{\mu}\right\|_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{2}_{z}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\mu^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\mu^{-s+\frac{1}{4}}c_{\mu}(0)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{7}{8}}\rho^{\frac{3}{4}}.

Estimate (
3.9
) follows from Hölder’s inequality, (
3.7
), and (
1.30
):

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\gtrsim\rho}\left\|L(P_{\nu}u_{i},P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j})\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\gtrsim\rho}\left\|P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{i}\right\|_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{\infty}_{z}}\left\|P_{\nu}u_{j}\right\|_{L^{4}_{t,y}L^{2}_{z}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\gtrsim\rho}\nu^{-\frac{1}{4}-\sigma}c_{\nu}(\sigma)(\nu^{\prime})^{-s+\frac{1}{4}}c_{\nu^{\prime}}(0)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{4}}\rho^{-s-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

which with (
1.27
), Hölder’s inequality, and Bernstein’s inequality yields (
3.10
):

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}(P_{\nu}u_{i}P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|P_{\rho}u_{l}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\|P_{\mu}(P_{\nu}u_{i}P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{t}}\|P_{\rho}u_{l}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{3}{2}}\|P_{\nu}u_{i}P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\|P_{\rho}u_{l}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma).

∎

We then collect several mixed-norm estimates derived primarily from the bilinear bootstrap bounds (
1.29
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Assume that (
1.27
), (
1.29
) and (
1.30
) hold. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{d}\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big\|\left\|P_{\mu}u_{i}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\left\|P_{\rho,\gamma}u_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{\gamma}}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{4}}\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(3.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\mu^{-1}\rho\sup_{\tau}\Big\|\|L(P_{\mu}u_{i},P_{\ll\mu}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{t,y}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-2s-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma),

(3.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sup_{\tau}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}(u_{i}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|P_{\rho,1}u_{l}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

(3.13)

where we set
y_{1}=x_{1}
in
y=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
,
i,j,l\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
.

Proof.

Estimate (
3.11
) follows directly from (
1.29
) and Bernstein’s inequality:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{d}\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big\|\left\|P_{\mu}u_{i}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\left\|P_{\rho,\gamma}u_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{\gamma}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{d}\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\big\|\left\|P_{\mu}u_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\left\|P_{\rho,\gamma}u_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{\gamma}}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{\gamma}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}\mu^{s-1}c_{\mu}(0)\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{4}}\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

where
\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}c_{\mu}(0)\lesssim\varepsilon
. Then, the (
3.11
) gives (
3.12
) immediately:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sup_{\tau}\mu^{-1}\rho\Big\|\|L(P_{\mu}u_{i},P_{\ll\mu}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{t,y}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\rho\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{\tau}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}u_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{x_{1}}}\cdot\sum_{\omega\ll\mu\ll\rho}\sup_{\tau^{\prime}}\Big\|\|P_{\omega}u_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}^{\tau^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{x_{1}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-2s-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma),

To prove (
3.13
), Bony decomposition gives

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sup_{\tau}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}(u_{i}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|P_{\rho,1}u_{l}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sup_{\tau}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}u_{i}P_{\ll\mu}u_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|P_{\rho,1}u_{l}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\,+\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\ll\rho}\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sup_{\tau}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}(P_{\nu}u_{i}P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|P_{\rho,1}u_{l}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}}

(3.15)

\displaystyle\,+\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sup_{\tau}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}(P_{\nu}u_{i}P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|P_{\rho,1}u_{l}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{t,y}},

(3.16)

where (
3.14
) can be estimated by (
3.11
) and (
3.4
) by taking infty norm of
P_{\ll\mu}u_{j}
, while (
3.16
) has been estimated in (
3.10
).
As for (
3.15
), we can also use (
3.11
) and (
3.4
) to obtain

\displaystyle\eqref{pre.bi3.t2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\ll\rho}\mu^{d-\frac{5}{2}}\sup_{\tau}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}(P_{\nu}u_{i}P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j})\|_{L^{1}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|P_{\rho,1}u_{l}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{1}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\ll\rho}\mu^{2s-\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{\tau}\Big\|\|P_{\nu}u_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|P_{\rho,1}u_{l}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x_{1}}}\cdot\|P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}L^{\infty}_{x_{1}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{8}}\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\ll\rho}\mu^{2s-\frac{1}{2}}\nu^{-s}c_{\nu}(0)\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma)(\nu^{\prime})^{-s+\frac{1}{2}}c_{\nu^{\prime}}(0)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-s-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma),

where
\sum_{\nu\ll\rho}c_{\nu}(0)\lesssim\varepsilon
.

∎

3.2.
Typical nonlinear estimates

We now estimate the nonlinear expressions that arise in the subsequent a priori estimates.

The first lemma establishes space–time estimates for the typical quadratic nonlinearity
u_{i}u_{j}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\rho}(u_{i}u_{j})\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{4}}\rho^{-s-\sigma}c_{\rho}(\sigma).

(3.17)

Proof.

The Bony’s decomposition yields

\displaystyle P_{\rho}\left(u_{i}u_{j}\right)=

\displaystyle P_{\ll\rho}u_{i}P_{\sim\rho}u_{j}+P_{\sim\rho}u_{i}P_{\ll\rho}u_{j}

(3.18)

\displaystyle+P_{\rho}\big(\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\gtrsim\rho}P_{\nu^{\prime}}u_{i}P_{\nu}u_{j}\big).

The low–high terms and high–low terms has been estimated by (
3.11
), while
the high–high terms follows directly from (
3.9
).

∎

We next estimate a delicate low–high interaction arising from the Bony decomposition of the nonlinear terms in the energy estimate. The argument exploits the special Gauss structure of
\lambda\wedge\lambda
. Its difference analogue will play an essential role in the weak difference estimates in dimension five; see the analysis of (
9.43
) in Section
9
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
d\geq 5
and
y_{1}=x_{1}
in
y=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\rho\mu^{-1}\sup_{\tau}\big\|\|P_{\mu}\left(\lambda\wedge\lambda\right)\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\big\|_{L^{1}_{t,y}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-2s-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma),

(3.19)

where
\lambda\wedge\lambda
is defined in (
1.11
).

Proof.

Applying Bony’s decomposition to
\lambda\wedge\lambda
, we obtain

\displaystyle P_{\mu}(\lambda\wedge\lambda)=

\displaystyle\,L\bigl(P_{\ll\mu}\lambda,P_{\mu}\lambda\bigr)+L\bigl(P_{\mu}\lambda,P_{\ll\mu}\lambda\bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\ll\rho}P_{\mu}L(P_{\nu}\lambda,P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda)+\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}P_{\mu}\bigl(\lambda_{\nu}\wedge\lambda_{\nu^{\prime}}\bigr).

(3.20)

Plugging the first two terms into (
3.19
), we can see it has been bounded by (
3.12
). The third term can be estimated by using (
1.29
) in a way similar to those for (
3.12
) as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\ll\rho}\rho\mu^{-1}\sup_{\tau}\left\|\|P_{\mu}L(P_{\nu}\lambda,P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda)\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\right\|_{L_{t,y}^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\ll\rho}\rho\mu^{d-2}\sup_{\tau}\left\|\|L(P_{\nu}\lambda,P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda)\|_{L_{\widehat{x}_{1}}^{1}}\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L_{\widehat{x}_{1}}^{2}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau}\|_{L_{\widehat{x}_{1}}^{2}}\right\|_{L_{t,x_{1}}^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-2s-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma).

As for the fourth term in (
3.20
), we need to exploit the additional structure given by (
1.11
). For each dyadic frequency
\nu
, write

\displaystyle P_{\nu}=\nu^{-1}\sum_{\beta=1}^{d}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{P}_{\nu,\beta},

(3.21)

where the multipliers
\widetilde{P}_{\nu,\beta}
are uniformly bounded.
By
u_{3}=\partial_{x}h
, the Codazzi equations (
1.7
) imply schematically

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}\lambda_{\gamma\sigma}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\sigma}\lambda_{\gamma\beta}+L\bigl((u_{3},A),\lambda\bigr),

(3.22)

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\lambda^{\alpha}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\sigma}\psi+L\bigl((u_{3},A),\lambda\bigr),

(3.23)

where we write
(u_{3},A)=u_{3}+A
for convenience.

For
\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho
, we obtain

\displaystyle P_{\mu}(\lambda_{\nu}\wedge\lambda_{\nu^{\prime}})=

\displaystyle\,\nu^{-1}P_{\mu}\left[\partial_{\sigma}\bigl(\widetilde{P}_{\nu,\beta}\lambda_{\gamma\beta}\bigr)\overline{\psi_{\nu^{\prime}}}-\partial_{\alpha}\bigl(\widetilde{P}_{\nu,\beta}\lambda_{\beta\gamma}\bigr)(P_{\nu^{\prime}}\overline{\lambda^{\alpha}_{\sigma}})\right]

\displaystyle+\nu^{-1}P_{\mu}L\left(P_{\nu}[\lambda\cdot(u_{3},A)],P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda\right).

Applying the product rule and using

\lambda_{\gamma\beta}=\lambda_{\beta\gamma}
, we further get

\displaystyle P_{\mu}(\lambda_{\nu}\wedge\lambda_{\nu^{\prime}})=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mu}{\nu}L(P_{\nu}\lambda,P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda)+\nu^{-1}P_{\mu}\left[\widetilde{P}_{\nu,\beta}\lambda_{\beta\gamma}\left(\partial_{\alpha}P_{\nu^{\prime}}\overline{\lambda^{\alpha}_{\sigma}}-\partial_{\sigma}\overline{\psi_{\nu^{\prime}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\nu^{-1}P_{\mu}L\left(P_{\nu}[\lambda\cdot(u_{3},A)],P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda\right),

which combining the contracted Codazzi equation (
3.23
)
yields

\displaystyle P_{\mu}(\lambda_{\nu}\wedge\lambda_{\nu^{\prime}})=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mu}{\nu}L(P_{\nu}\lambda,P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda)+\nu^{-1}P_{\mu}L\left(P_{\nu}[\lambda\cdot(u_{3},A)],\lambda_{\nu^{\prime}}\right)

(3.24)

\displaystyle+\nu^{-1}P_{\mu}L\left(P_{\nu^{\prime}}[\lambda\cdot(u_{3},A)],\lambda_{\nu}\right).

Thus the quadratic high–high interaction gains the factor

\mu/\nu
, while the remaining terms are cubic.

Putting the first term in right hand side of in
(
3.24
) into (
3.19
), Bernstein’s inequality and (
1.30
) yield

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\rho\mu^{-1}\sup_{\tau}\left\|\|\frac{\mu}{\nu}\cdot P_{\mu}L(P_{\nu}\lambda,P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda)\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\right\|_{L_{t,y}^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\rho\mu^{d-3}\nu^{-1}\|P_{\nu}\lambda\|_{L_{t,y}^{4}L_{z}^{2}}\cdot\|P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda\|_{L_{t,y}^{4}L_{z}^{2}}\cdot\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L_{t,y}^{4}L_{z}^{2}}\cdot\|\psi_{\rho,1}\|_{L_{t,y}^{4}L_{z}^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-2s-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma)\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\left(\frac{\mu}{\rho}\right)^{d-3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-2s-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma),

where
d\geq 5
, and thus the last sum is bounded.

Finally, the cubic terms we only need to consider
L\left(P_{\nu^{\prime}}(\lambda u_{3}),\lambda_{\nu}\right)
as follows, as the rest is the same.

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\rho(\mu\nu)^{-1}\Big\|\|P_{\mu}L\left(P_{\nu^{\prime}}(\lambda u_{3}),\lambda_{\nu}\right)\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\Big\|_{L_{t,y}^{1}}

(3.25)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\rho\mu^{d-\frac{5}{2}}\nu^{-1}\|P_{\mu}L\left(P_{\nu^{\prime}}(\lambda\cdot u_{3}),P_{\nu}\lambda\right)\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{1}_{x}}\cdot\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L_{t,y}^{4}L_{z}^{2}}\cdot\|\psi_{\rho,1}\|_{L_{t,y}^{4}L_{z}^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\rho\mu^{d-\frac{5}{2}}\nu^{-1}\left\|P_{\nu^{\prime}}(\lambda\cdot u_{3})\right\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\left\|P_{\nu^{\prime}}\lambda\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\cdot\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}\|_{L_{t,y}^{4}L_{z}^{2}}\cdot\|\psi_{\rho,1}\|_{L_{t,y}^{4}L_{z}^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sum_{\nu\sim\nu^{\prime}\gtrsim\rho}\rho\mu^{d-\frac{5}{2}}\nu^{-1}\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{4}}(\nu^{\prime})^{-s}c_{\nu^{\prime}}(0)\cdot\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{8}}\nu^{-s}c_{\nu}(0)\cdot\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}\rho^{-2s+\frac{1}{2}-2\sigma}c^{2}_{\rho}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\rho^{-2s-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma).

This completes the proof of this Lemma.

∎

An analogue of Lemma
3.5
also holds for cubic terms.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
y_{1}=x_{1}
in
y=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
,
i,j,l\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
and
d\geq 4
. Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sup_{\tau,\tau^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\,\rho\mu^{-2}\left\|\left\|P_{\mu}\left(u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\left\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}^{\tau}\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\right\|_{L^{1}_{t,y}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-2s-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma).

(3.26)

Proof.

Apply Bony’s decomposition to the product
(u_{i}u_{j})u_{l}
:

\displaystyle P_{\mu}\left(u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}\right)=

\displaystyle\,P_{\ll\mu}(u_{i}u_{j})P_{\mu}u_{l}+P_{\mu}(u_{i}u_{j})P_{\ll\mu}u_{l}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\,+\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\ll\rho}P_{\mu}\big(P_{\nu^{\prime}}(u_{i}u_{j})P_{\nu}u_{l}\big)+\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\gtrsim\rho}P_{\mu}\big(P_{\nu^{\prime}}(u_{i}u_{j})P_{\nu}u_{l}\big).

The low–high term and high–low term can be estimated similarly, and we will only estimate the high low. Putting the low–high term into (
3.6
), we can use (
3.11
) and (
3.13
) to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sup_{\tau,\tau^{\prime}}\rho\mu^{-2}\left\|\left\|P_{\ll\mu}(u_{i}u_{j})P_{\mu}u_{l}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\left\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}^{\tau}\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\right\|_{L^{1}_{t,y}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\rho\sum_{\omega\ll\mu\ll\rho}\sup_{\tau}\mu^{-\frac{3}{2}}\big\|\|P_{\omega}(u_{i}u_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}^{\tau}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\big\|_{L^{1}_{t,x_{1}}}

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{\mu\ll\rho}\sup_{\tau^{\prime}}\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\|P_{\mu}u_{l}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\right\|_{L^{1}_{t,x_{1}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\rho^{-2s-2\sigma}c_{\rho}^{2}(\sigma).

Similarly for the third term in (
3.27
), we can put it into (
3.6
), estimate it by using (
1.29
) in a way similar to those for (
3.11
) and (
3.13
) as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\ll\rho}\sup_{\tau,\tau^{\prime}}\rho\mu^{-2}\left\|\left\|P_{\mu}\big(P_{\nu^{\prime}}(u_{i}u_{j})P_{\nu}u_{l}\big)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\left\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}^{\tau}\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}_{z}}\right\|_{L^{1}_{t,y}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\mu\lesssim\nu^{\prime}\sim\nu\ll\rho}\sup_{\tau,\tau^{\prime}}\rho\mu^{d-3}\left\|\big\|P_{\mu}\big(P_{\nu^{\prime}}(u_{i}u_{j})P_{\nu}u_{l}\big)\right\|_{L^{1}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\left\|\psi_{\sim\rho,1}^{\tau}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\|\psi_{\rho,1}^{\tau^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}_{\widehat{x}_{1}}}\big\|_{L^{1}_{t,x_{1}}}
